**@mardukdavid3222** (👍0 · 2022-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CsFtVU7f_yc&lc=UgwaVpWHRL5MFPajDDR4AaABAg
  Hi Adam .Great work ,Keep going.
  I have been into Etymology of English words.Interestingly many of English words evolved from Tamil words.Just sharing a few.
  "Ari Mathipu" became Arithmetic.
  "Ari"(அறி) mean Know and   "Mathipu" (மதிப்பு)  mean Value.Arithmetic mean Know Value.
  "Pala" became Plus > Ply > Poly.
  Pala(பல) mean Many.
  "Maga Mathipu" became Mathematic
  Mathematic mean Great/Big Value.
  Maga(மகா) mean Great/ Big > Mega
  Etu(ஏத்து) Mathipu(மதிப்பு) became Estimate.
  Etu mean Increase  and Mathipu mean Value.
  Estimate mean Increase Value.
  The word "Value" is from "Vilai"
  Vilai(விலை) mean Value.
  Kal kulaithal(கல் குழைத்தல் ) became Calculate .
  Kal(கல்) mean Pebble and Kulaitjal is Mix.
  Calculate mean Counting of Mixed Pebbles.
  Di Vedu became Divide .
  Di mean two and Veddu mean Chop/ Cut.
  Divide mean Cut into 2.

  Hi Adam, if possible, please also explain the difference between "in which and where"  "at/on which and when" in your upcoming videos. Is it correct to say: "The  book where she discusses..."  instead of "The  book in which she discusses... "?
    ↳ **@fredylopez2477** (👍3 · 2022-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CsFtVU7f_yc&lc=Ugx6hvfRtBLUh8PexdJ4AaABAg.9bnbok-VkNw9bngNrYnhs_
      - the book where she discusses = there's a specific book WHERE we're talking about something and we mention that something in the whole book.
      
      - the book in which she discusses = there's a specific book IN WHICH we're talking about something in a specidic part of the book.
      - Well that's the way I see it.
      I would say that both sentences convey the same idea, that's to say they're synonyms.

  I worked in the US for a number of years on an international team comprising Japanese, Russian, Korean, Italian, French, German, Austrian, Swedish, Spanish, and American scientists. Funny fact, we could easily understand everyone else despite our very strong and different accents and frequent grammatical errors, but we often could not understand the American fellows speaking proper English. This is frequently misportrayed in movies, where the English-speaker main characters speak to foreign characters who, despite their very poor and broken English, seem to understand them without any problem. I think that for anyone with a low/intermediate level in a foreign language, it is much easier to get understood than to understand native speakers. I bet it's much easier for an English speaker to understand another fellow English speaker speaking broken Spanish than to understand a conversation between two Spaniards.

  11:13 "Eventually, you will find the actual meaning"
  But sometimes this "eventually" takes several months.
  For example, I heard a word "igzaiti" several times when I was listening to the radio, but I could not find this word in any dictionaries.   "I have been teaching English twenty two years on."; I have written this sentence, replacing "I have been teaching English for twenty two years."

    ↳ **@armandkoko6096** (👍2 · 2022-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=O5rF6kvyu1g&lc=UgwWYz2hzYZrzmyFyQR4AaABAg.9Yyqa7nVJo69YytUuNPnOs
      Read a lot. Specially drama books and so forth. I also suggest you to have a native penfriend to talk to usually. It could help.
    ↳ **@silviomp** (👍0 · 2022-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=O5rF6kvyu1g&lc=UgwWYz2hzYZrzmyFyQR4AaABAg.9Yyqa7nVJo69Yywn8JJdDG
      Well, I tell my brain I'm not speaking another language, it's still my first language and it's all synonyms for the words I already speak. Repeat every new synonym out loud over and over again until it becomes natural, until you feel what you mean. Do in English exactly as you do when you learn a new word in your first language. Don't ask questions like "why this word comes first?" Just pretend you're a native child and accept the way words appear in a sentence. Children love repetitions and learning new stuff. They even ask you to tell them the same story, the same joke. Their favorite word is "again." Don't learn English, live English. If you can't move to a English speaking country, try to do everything in English where you are. Wake up in English, brush your teeth in English, take a dump and flush the toilet in English. Watch movies, series, home videos on YouTube. The "magic" doesn't happen overnight. Repeat every new word/phrase/idiom/sentence until you feel like it's your native language. Do it everyday and you'll see the results.

  5:00 Isn't the sentence ambiguous?
  1. Ask him (what?) so that he finds out what she wanted, or
  2. Ask him (why?) so that you find out what she wanted.
  In the first case the infinitive clause is the object of 'ask', the thing we want him to do. In the second case it's the reason why you should ask him. The first meaning is probably the intended one but I'm not sure if the second meaning is also possible.

      @fredylopez2477  if we all remember that remember and forget are the verb that need gerung,Ving,to come after them to show the regret that we did or didn't do something in the past like opposite situation in the present.With that, we can also do like I forget bringing my camera,which is the same your 2 sentences.Are we on the same page or agreeing?
    ↳ **@fredylopez2477** (👍0 · 2022-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=exmDE3cMU24&lc=UgwGlkm6rl0S3Ap9Drl4AaABAg.9X0hTOGn4RS9X20Vs4VeIM
      @phumkiatwananuraksakul7815  Well, first of all, with all respect,  I'm not typing that you don't know how to type or write in English but I really don't understand what you're trying to mean but let me answer to what I did understand ;
      
      Actually; "to remember" is one the verbs you can use the infinitive OR the gerund with the next verb or the verb that follows, but the meaning changes a little bit, example;
      
      - Remember TO TURN OFF the light. (You have to turn the light off before leaving).
      
      - I remember TURNING OFF the light. (You're sure you turned the light before you left, I mean, this is about the past)
      
      - Now, if I'm not wrong "to forget" is one of the verbs you can use ONLY  the infinitive with the next verb or the verb that follows, example;
      - I forgot TO tell you the truth.
      If you say "I forgot TELLING you the truth", this is incorrect, as far as I know, and I know that Gerund of the verbs has several functions, like, acting as a subject, example ;
      -" SWIMMING is my favorite sport."
    ↳ **@fredylopez2477** (👍0 · 2022-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=exmDE3cMU24&lc=UgwGlkm6rl0S3Ap9Drl4AaABAg.9X0hTOGn4RS9X27KZyQN41
      @phumkiatwananuraksakul7815  Hi pal, it's me again, well, one more thing my friend, we can use the verb "Forget" with gerund too, but, like I typed, the meaning changes a little bit, example;
      - I forgot TO close the door (definite action in the past). You're sure.
      
      - I forgot CLOSING the door. (having closed the door, maybe you forgot but you're not sure) I hope I made myself understood. but, usually, the infinitive must be used in the verbs following this one (forget) as an example ;
      - "Don't forget TO BRING the documents" Thanks for minding my comments.

**@astronautid7948** (👍0 · 2022-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=V1_NotVTseU&lc=UgysgJ8q6AbjIo5ao2N4AaABAg
  I'm wondering what are chances for each of these words to stay. Where do you think words like "g.o.a.t." will be in like 10 years? Here's my take on it.
  BAI is an acronym  , it's quick to type it. So people will use it -- and it won't disappear without a trace.
  Goals in this particular sense will hardly stand the test of time because things in the social media space change most rapidly. Expect users to find a replacement for it in the blink of an eye.
  Goat, the Greatest Of All Times, must stay. It's so characteristic of English to use irony. In sports, a goat initially was a person to blame for the team failure. G.o.a.t will complement it so you get a nice play on words. Besides, there's a story behind g.o.a.t., that Adam touched on. I heard the story goes as far back as Muhammad Ali -- without him there would not be any g.o.a.t. So as long as sports are played the word will likely be part of English.
  To throw a shadow, may or may not stay. It depends. Dictionaries will most likely list it, but the usage will drop off as celebrities and those covering their lives are on the lookout for new fresh expressions. Thumbing a nose at someone can be done in many ways. So the future will see many other verbal expressions of doing it rise.
  Now onto "snowflake", and "gaslight". Just because these have gained traction doesn't mean they'll stick around.
  There will be room in dictionaries for phrases like "don't be like a snowflake under the first sign of heat". But, again, English is more than full -- full to the brims -- with ways to say just that. Snowflake has just as much of a chance as a snowball in hell.
  Gaslight. This is one negative word. Few know that the word is heavily loaded with its historical meaning. It is so negative lawsuits can be filed against those using it too much. In the realm of politics any journalist or pundit worth their salt is bound to come up with new words to keep a sense of proportion. On the one side it bears a colossal charge of negativity to get the audience tired of it. On the other when someone undermines your sense of reality to a point of sanity, the word is just a fit.
  Now, being triggered seems to be a new norm. The word has its uses, definitely, as people are reactive and oftentimes feel offended by many things. But the usage will get more low-key.
  Onto the next. Meme describes a thing that is a fact of life in an age of Internet. It's not going to go.
  The same goes for "snub". Oftentimes friends are a thing we get too much of and we snub them. The word seems to have been around since ancient times.
  Woke. As long as folks are aware of injustice, wrongdoings and discrimination and speak up they're woke.
  Binge has taken root. Again, it's a thing we see, a specific type of behavior.
  As to "ghost" I don't know what to make of it. What are your thoughts, guys? Adam did a good job explaining these words currently in the spotlight.
   
  In my analysis there wasn't any rule of thumb. I just went with my gut. So I might be way off the mark. What do you think? Feel free to disagree, it won't trigger me. i'm looking forward to reading your comments to see what you, guys, think about the prospects of these words.

**@pequodexpress** (👍0 · 2024-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3zQVJ-rffAI&lc=Ugy76B0_5N1wIj0v_e94AaABAg
  There is a valid reason to use the definite article in sentences such as "The lion is a fierce animal" and "The whale is the largest mammal."
  
  
  The use of the definite article "the" in the sentence "The lion is a fierce animal" serves to emphasize the lion as a representative of its species or as a conceptual archetype. The emphasis lies not on a particular individual lion but on the lion as a general, recognized entity.
  
  Emphasis on Representation:
  
  "The lion" suggests a lion as a symbol or embodiment of its kind. It's not just any lion; it's the lion, representing the species as a whole. This usage can be found in discussions or descriptions where the focus is on the characteristics or qualities commonly associated with lions.
  Emphasis on the Generic Concept:
  
  By saying "The lion is a fierce animal," there is an implication that lions, as a category, are known for their fierceness. The definite article, in this case, emphasizes the conceptual idea of a lion as a fierce creature rather than any specific lion.
  Emphasis on Universality:
  
  The use of "the" can also convey a sense of universality. It implies that what is being said about lions applies to lions in general, reinforcing a broader statement about the nature of the species.

  I disagree on this one.
  
  "societies that embrace it"
  Is synonymous with:
   "[any] societies that embrace it".
  Therefore the definite article in this case is optional rather than required.
  A relative clause merely modifies a noun, and does not necessarily renders it definite. Much like an adjective. That's why sometimes it is also called an "adjectival clause".
